<h4>The World Does Not Understand</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/Afinetheorem/status/2107658843996000292">The AIs all say when asked about all these math proofs as a hypothetical that this would be a huge deal, and should be front page new</a>s, historic beyond any reasonable comparison, the biggest day in mathematics. Some think it is impossible.</p>
<p>It turns out this was not front page news. Most people did not hear about it. It should have been, but no one in the news business knows what it means, or thinks people would care.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Afinetheorem/status/2107634914493710478">Kevin A. Bryan</a>: Ok, finished going through the OAI math list. It is bonkers and should be front page news around the world if folks understood what this meant. But if I am running strategy at a lab, #1 priority is “do this for medicine, oncology, battery efficiency, etc as fast as possible”.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://joshuagans.substack.com/p/openai-drops-a-bomb-on-maths?r=oc6a&amp;utm_campaign=post&amp;utm_medium=web">Joshua Gans starts off his coverage with the actual front page</a>, in order to show us what things The New York Times thought were more important than solving 90 of the top 500 open math problems at once.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eyqxBuxXCaamNSxBB/yv9tbzqqukpw912qq6k5" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/SethBurn/status/2107819367362887829">Seth Burn</a>: Sometimes front page news doesn’t initially reach the front page. The best example is Sputnik. It launched 10/4/57, but the Soviets didn’t think it was a huge deal [and it got a few paragraphs of a right-side column].</p>
<p><a href="https://cosmo.org/for-some-sputnik-1-was-no-big-deal-until-it-was/">It was only after America freaked out that it [became a banner headline] in Russia on 10/6/57</a>.</p></blockquote>
<p>It was a remarkably slow news day otherwise. Much of this is not exactly breaking. Yet no math is to be seen, even in the summaries at the bottom, which even include a generic AI thinkpiece.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://joshuagans.substack.com/p/openai-drops-a-bomb-on-maths?r=oc6a">Joshua Gans</a>: What’s missing is this: <a href="https://github.com/openai/math">722 mathematics papers written by OpenAI</a>. It isn’t an overstatement to say that this is probably the biggest day of scientific advancement in history. It is hard to evaluate, but the discussion that I have been seeing is that many of these are among the hardest and most significant results in mathematics. I suspect October 6th, 2026, will go down as some form of Judgment Day for AI in mathematics, but it portends so much more.</p></blockquote>
<p>The counterargument is that there might be a lot of days like this:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2107914863129559271">roon</a> (OpenAI): will yesterday be remembered as an important day? hard to say. in a punctuated exponential every local maximum looks invisible from a bit further out</p></blockquote>


<h4>For Now You Can Still Do Math</h4>


<p>I like this model of why mathematics is not done quite yet:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://joshuagans.substack.com/p/openai-drops-a-bomb-on-maths?r=oc6a">Joshua Gans</a>: The mathematicians are now going through this. To which I offer again the above Bookend slide to help:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eyqxBuxXCaamNSxBB/c2tzfuanbmm2vcf9v14e" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>We cannot yet show that the AI is better at conjectures or final verification. That will take some additional time, although probably not all that much time.</p>
<p>Verification has to be pretty good or we would have found a lot more errors by now. There are a lot of mathematicians who would love to find a mistake. We know, via the reasoning traces, that a big chunk of effort is going to verification.</p>


<h4>You Will Need To Find A New Problem</h4>


<p>Scott Aaronson reports on the Mathocalypse.</p>
<blockquote><p>Scott Aaronson (<a href="https://scottaaronson.blog/?p=10169">Shtetl-Optimized</a>): Last night my 9-year-old son was taunting my wife, complexity theorist <a href="https://www.cs.utexas.edu/~danama/">Dana Moshkovitz</a>, as follows: “mommy, I heard you got <em>cooked</em>! I heard that a <em>robot</em> solved the math problem you worked on for your whole career! OOF!”</p>
<p>While my son was being a brat, he also wasn’t wrong.</p>
<p>Whether you’re thrilled, depressed, angry, or whatever else about it, yesterday was surely one of the biggest days in mathematical history.</p>
<p>And yes, among the 372 huge results <a href="https://openai.com/index/sharing-ai-progress-in-mathematics/">released yesterday by OpenAI</a>, on the recommendation of its <a href="https://agmai.org/">advisory group</a> of Timothy Gowers, Edward Witten, and other distinguished mathematicians, was a <a href="https://github.com/openai/math/blob/main/preprints/The-Unique-Games-Theorem-September-23-2026/paper.pdf">proof</a> of Subhash Khot’s <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unique_games_conjecture">Unique Games Conjecture (UGC)</a>, a statement that my wife has worked toward proving for the entire time I’ve known her.</p></blockquote>
<p>Then there is the other kind of ‘new problem,’ where those in denial, who three years ago took comfort in talking about how LLMs could not do math, have to find a new way to explain why anything an AI can do is not real or not meaningful.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://scottaaronson.blog/?p=10169">Shtetl-Optimized</a>:</p>
<p>Experience has shown that, <em>even now</em>, there will still be people explaining in patronizing tones why none of this is real and none of it counts. If such people were capable of being impressed by <em>anything</em> that happens in the empirical world, of updating on <em>anything</em>, they would’ve already been impressed and already updated several years ago, long before things had reached the point of an actual Mathocalypse.</p>
<p>So, they’ll say, maybe the alleged solutions are not solutions at all, but just “AI slop.” Or maybe none of the 372 well-known open problems that were solved were <em>real</em> math problems, they were all just glorified contest puzzles and trivialities. (After all, there’s still no Riemann Hypothesis!) Or maybe the entire 4000-year-old discipline of mathematics needs to be jettisoned: turns out that it was <em>all</em> just puzzle-solving and trivialities; all that’s different is that now the triviality stands unmasked. In any case, what really matters is that the <em>true</em> inner sanctum of human creativity hasn’t been breached and probably never will be, and also, that Sam Altman and Dario Amodei are contemptible little nerds.</p>
<p>If you’re still a proponent of that doomed worldview, still aboard the sinking ship, I encourage you in the strongest possible terms to read yesterday’s <em>other</em> great contribution to AI discourse, besides the OpenAI Mathocalypse dump: namely, <a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/an-open-letter-to-steven-pinker-on">Scott Alexander’s open letter to Steven Pinker</a>.</p></blockquote>
<p>I do have to tip my hat to the fully general counterargument to all possible AI hype:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/StephenLCasper/status/2108216346505089173">Cas</a>: I think that AI smashing human achievements in mathematics might be overhyped, more an example of Moravec’s paradox than a robust sign of singularity soon. Recall how AI eclipsed humans at Go ~10 years ago but was much slower to get good at tasks that would change the world.</p></blockquote>
<p>Whatever AI is good at is Moravec’s paradox. Whatever AI is bad at is why it sucks. Why are you so excited that AI is suddenly superhuman in an increasingly large set of domains, including the exact things we were saying it was dumb for being bad at? Surely this will not extend soon to other domains.</p>


<h4>Verification or Evaluation Is Not Always Easier Than Generation</h4>


<p>The steelman of this critique is to draw a distinction between verifiable versus unverifiable domains.</p>
<p>This argument says that whenever you have a verified domain, with known ground truth, AI will quickly become superhuman.</p>
<p>Whereas, if verification is difficult, or all you can do is evaluate in an informal way that requires a human in the loop to avoid biased errors and distorted behaviors, then AI capabilities will lag behind.</p>
<p>We were in a weird place for a few years, when pretraining dominated and LLMs were mostly trained on human words. This led to a seemingly non-Yudkowsky world in key ways, with LLMs being relatively excellent at a variety of useful but unverified domains, while being hopeless at math.</p>
<p>Now, with post-training dominating, LLMs are again stronger at math and coding, and things again look like you would expect. AI is quickly getting better at everything, but progress in other domains, while super fast compared to almost any other tech ever, is relatively slow for now. Thus, we advance math and coding and similar domains first, which then automate AI R&amp;D, which then accelerates everything else.</p>


<h4>Cracking the Code</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/drakefjustin/status/2107837081313505768">Justin Drake notices that there is a striking under-representation of cryptographic breakthroughs</a> among the results. As in, zero of the 719 abstracts mention cryptography, LWE or discrete logs.</p>
<p>One option is that AI is relatively weak at cryptography, another is luck or that OpenAI didn’t have cryptography in its initial problem set (perhaps to avoid exactly this issue?), or perhaps the government or OpenAI are censoring those results.</p>
<p>Justin calls for a ‘bunker mode’ for the blockchain industry, to protect against sudden mass breakthroughs.</p>
<p>Vitalik says not to panic, <a href="https://x.com/VitalikButerin/status/2107976296320106851">but that there are serious risks to cryptography from potential new math discoveries</a>, including to lattices but not yet to hashes.</p>
<blockquote><p>Vitalik Buterin: But for anything that has structure, you should assume that AI will make at least some progress in breaking that structure. Here, one reasonable inference is that if you want to make something plausibly long-term secure, multiply the key sizes by 10.</p>
<p>… Theoretically, of course it’s possible that hashes are broken too (eg. P = NP would imply that). But I think P = NP is very unlikely.</p>
<p>And intuitively, it’s much more likely that a mathematical object has exactly no exploitable structure (like hashes are intended to), than that a mathematical object has exactly ~3 forms of exploitable structure (for elliptic curves: associativity, Schoof, pairings) and not some secret fourth form of structure we have not yet discovered that greatly degrades its security (for elliptic curves, ECDLP and pairing security). Similar for LWE, SVP, RLWE and the zoo of lattice problems.</p>
<p>For this reason, we do not yet see any reason to worry and start padding the byte size of hashes (if we start to worry more, we would pad the round count first before doing anything to the byte size).</p>
<p>Concrete TLDR, my own personal views:</p>
<p>* Hash-based &gt; lattice-based, in those situations where hash-based is possible at all<br />
* For anything lattice-based, be much more paranoid on param sizes. Remember that blockchains are only a small portion of the cryptography story; this point goes far beyond blockchains and applies to eg. access to websites, secure messaging, Tor / VPNs …<br />
* For privacy protocols, strongly favor NOT putting encrypted notes onchain. Instead, send them offchain through some third-party mechanism.<br />
* If it’s not difficult for you, keeping your funds in addresses which have not yet been used to make a transaction is a good idea. If it’s easy for you, do it. **But be careful about migrations; I personally have lost more money in botched migrations than I have lost in all hacks combined**.<br />
* For multisig wallets, doing confirmations offchain is better than onchain, because this way the signatures of signer wallets do not get exposed to the public, so if ECDSA falls to AI much faster than expected, at least the multisig “gracefully degrades” to a 1-of-1 where the 1 is whoever was gathering the signatures – a much better place to be than “anyone can take the money”</p></blockquote>
<p>I am guessing Vitalik’s point about botched migrations is highly underappreciated. It is very easy, in crypto, to lose fantastical amounts of money from stupid mistakes, the same way you can lose it from hacks or tech failures.</p>
<p>Yes, you should worry, especially given that OpenAI’s breakthroughs here did not involve trying maximally hard.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/kmad/status/2107971206208995605">Kevin Madura</a>: given the math announcements how many novel attacks on cryptography do we think exist now but are unreleased? What does this look like in 1 year? 5 years?</p>
<p>I’d be eyeing DoD / NSA / NIST guidance pretty closely now</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/matthew_d_green/status/2107992246360649879">Matthew Green</a>: I think we might lose public key cryptography.</p>
<p>Well, encryption specifically.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Never Change</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/GaryMarcus/status/2107644352021639447">Gary Marcus is defending his position by saying</a> that the neural networks involved had a separate symbolic system, <a href="https://x.com/GaryMarcus/status/2107666238545723869">so none of this counts and he was right all along</a>, and if ‘that’s over your head’ you probably shouldn’t be commenting on AI. The polite response is that the distinction does not matter in practice.</p>


<h4>The Mathematicians Are Not Okay</h4>


<p><a href="https://proofsandprompts.com/2026/10/08/100-reactions-to-100-solutions/">Here are 100+ reactions from various different people</a> in mathematics. A lot of them are very not happy about how OpenAI handled this, especially that so many of the papers were ‘unreadable slop’ rather than having been made nice first, or that OpenAI solved these problems at all. If you want to know ‘what are the mathematicians thinking’ this is a great resource.</p>
<p>Here is Terence Tao’s serious response, which focuses on how this disrupts the work:</p>
<blockquote><p>Terence Tao: My feelings on recent developments are very mixed and complex.</p>
<p>On the one hand, many of the AI-generated proofs appear to introduce clever new ideas that will be fruitful once digested, while also building upon the existing contributions of countless human mathematicians past and present. But at the same time, I am deeply frustrated that, in sharp contrast to traditional breakthroughs, none of the humans involved in these proofs are available to take questions, give talks, attend conferences, submit papers to journals, train students, or otherwise participate in the subsequent development of these results.</p>
<p>Similarly, I am excited by the possibility of the community being able to use these tools to tackle ambitious and large-scale projects that one could not have even dreamed of in the past. But I am horrified by the many person-years of ongoing patient and deliberately slow research efforts – particularly by graduate students and postdocs – towards many motivating problems in mathematics being casually disrupted or destroyed by such a release. Much as one cannot unhear a movie spoiler or a crossword clue, one cannot explore a problem as profitably and richly once one is aware of an existing solution. Yes, one can still analyze and digest such an answer; but the best opportunity to do so is at the moment of its discovery, and such moments are increasingly wasted when delegated entirely to AI tools.</p>
<p>And I mourn the path not taken, and the opportunities lost in the frantic race to develop this technology. Labs submitting their frontier models to independent researchers for proper scientific evaluation. Coordination with the research community to ensure these tools are applied to complement and enhance the abilities and activities of human researchers, rather than compete with them. Use of these tools to foster collaboration and sharing, rather than competition and secrecy. Opening new doors, without closing old ones.</p>
<p>But that is not the path we now find ourselves in. Instead, the community needs to come together more than ever. To clearly declare our own standards and values, to build our own tools and practices, to support our most vulnerable members, and to chart our own path forward. Let’s get to work.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/GaryMarcus/status/2107859368666689545">Here is a less polite version:</a></p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eyqxBuxXCaamNSxBB/fymnjita5vlpl73ezu8u" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Here is another report from mathematician-land. People do not seem thrilled.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/notquantized/status/2108270190114824634">β/σi</a>: &gt; worried about their future<br />
&gt; worried about the solutions that **haven’t** been published (hello cryptography)<br />
&gt; in disbelief about the capabilities of these models<br />
&gt; sad the parts they fell in love with are gone (like we saw in swe)<br />
&gt; go solve “real” problems in bio (hilarious coming from mathematicians btw)</p>
<p>ps this is just my small sample, i only have so many mathematician friends</p></blockquote>
<p>Mathematicians seem to think of math as their playground, and rather than be happy about the problems being solved they feel their toys are being taken away. There is a struggle between wanting to know, and wanting to try to solve, and wanting the credit, and wanting solutions to be found at all. What do you actually care about most?</p>
<p>To be smart enough to be a mathematician, and also choose to be a mathematician instead of the many other better-paying things you could do, at least kind of requires some of the attitude that leads to cartoons where someone tells the mathematician someone found an application for their work, and the mathematician panics. Definitely #NotAllMathematicians, of course.</p>
<p>I am highly sympathetic. I really am. There could I have gone. That attitude has great value, not only inherently, but also exactly because following such curiosity leads to some of the most valuable discoveries that you would otherwise miss.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/WKCosmo/status/2106009807803031909">Will Kinney</a>: One thing I’m really starting to notice is that the theoretical physics community is responding to the disruption created by AI in a completely different way than the mathematics community has.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hsu_steve/status/2108497331347689813/history">steve hsu</a>: If your life’s work is a truly important problem – like curing cancer or fusion energy or discovering the true nature of quantum reality – then suddenly getting a solution from AI would be cause for great celebration.</p>
<p>You might ponder for a moment the second order impact on your profession, but that would be overwhelmed by JOY for the gift that you and the rest of humanity have received.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eyqxBuxXCaamNSxBB/ufp2awk6iv2b24nff5wm" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>It is also totally reasonable to focus on how things impact you and yours. That’s what hits home, and it is the part you can most control and are forced to face.</p>
<blockquote><p>Josh Frisch: Like many other mathematicians, my main emotion thus far in 2026 has been loss: loss of meaning, loss of purpose, loss of the era of human proofs, loss of the ability to picture the future. With the release yesterday, October 6, 2026, of hundreds of beautiful results—many, maybe most, answering someone’s “one question”—I am trying to move beyond loss. There are so many beautiful results here: problems nobody had any approaches for, algorithms nobody thought could possibly exist, unexpected isomorphisms, constructions and proofs.​</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2108383554879299908">Andrew Curran</a>: Please resist the urge to mock the mathematicians struggling through this moment. Whatever your field, your expertise, your passion, you too shall one day experience what they are going through. And when you remember your words, you will feel remorse.</p></blockquote>
<p>If your response is ‘well everyone’s passions are going away and the AIs come for us all’ then that’s worse. You know why that’s worse, right? Nor does it make this easier. There are some people out there reacting to these understandable reactions in truly vile ways. I urge them to stop.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.quantamagazine.org/is-ai-the-end-of-math-as-we-know-it-20261005/">Mathematics is facing a real problem here</a>. If the AIs prove all the theorems, then our current methods of getting good at understanding math stop working. The traditional way you understand problems is by working to solve them and a solution is often not worth so much if no one understands it:</p>
<blockquote><p>Jordana Cepelewicz (Quanta Magazine): It’s as if you were teleported to the peak of a tall mountain. Surrounded by fog, you have no idea where you are, or what’s around you. You do not know how your mountain connects to others, and you have no equipment to help you explore, no way to help someone else join you. If you had climbed the mountain yourself, you would have experienced how the human body adapts to altitude and changes in oxygen levels. You might have had to invent tools to navigate, to climb steep cliffs, or to make a shelter. You might have encountered a fellow explorer, gotten lost together in a hidden valley, and found a plant that could be turned into a life-saving medicine.</p>
<p>Instead you’re perched on the peak but in the dark, while the maker of the teleportation machine tells you that it can explore the wilderness better than any human.</p></blockquote>
<p>There are other ways to train understanding, but it could take a while to develop them, and they will require more motivation, especially intrinsic motivation.</p>
<p>This also destroys the system of PhDs and postdocs, since the problem you are looking to solve or the grant you want can get pulled out from under you at any time.</p>
<p>This also is a not fun place to be:</p>
<blockquote><p>Many mathematicians have stopped posting open conjectures and potential ideas at the end of their papers, for fear that they’ll be scraped by bots and fed into AI models. Others are posting their papers before they’re ready in order to avoid getting scooped.</p></blockquote>


<h4>The Situation Turns Ugly</h4>


<p>Some solutions were elegant and pretty cool. For example, the 9/4 matrix multiplication paper is 13 pages and bounds Strassen’s spectral characters directly. We’ve seen a bunch of people, after seeing their favorite problem get solved, share their ‘aha’ moment reading the proof.</p>
<p>Other solutions were less elegant.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/driftingwendigo/status/2107653100471287857">I am with Wendigo that it is kind of weird and terrible</a> that we are seeing all these new tiny improvements to previously elegant bounds on calculations, that have almost zero practical benefit.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AcerFur/status/2107606747972309163">Acer</a>: oh yeah btw guys we can do integer multiplication faster than n log n lol</p>
<p><a href="https://github.com/openai/math/tree/main/preprints/Integer-multiplication-below-n-log-n-September-23-2026">I was definitely very surprised when this one came in lol</a></p>

The sublime beauty of math is looking like a weak approximation resulting from our limited human faculties.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/uubzu/status/2107671922498830455">Uubzu v4</a>: I think the AI is just fucking with us when it takes an elegant bound like n(lg n) and improves it by 1/2^182. Like thanks for nothing buddy.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2108026972022718890">Now how about if we push it down to (1/2)^59?</a> Then to (1/2)^34? <a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2108248745997640107">And then (1/2)^14</a>?</p>

<p>κ = 2⁻³⁴ (tightened from κ = 2⁻¹<sup>8</sup>²)</p>
<p>The exact witness is 8.3 × 10⁻¹¹, a roughly 48 million fold improvement over the previous result and a 2¹⁴⁸ fold improvement over original OAI result.</p>

<h4>The Advisory Group Responds</h4>


<p>Here is the full statement by the leading advisory group:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://agmai.org/#openai-release">agmai.org</a>: As announced a few weeks ago, OpenAI has released a large collection of mathematical results generated by an internal model, reporting solutions to hundreds of open questions. This is an important event for mathematics, with consequences both for mathematics and for the mathematical community that extend far beyond the individual results.</p>
<p>AGMAI’s advisory role should not be interpreted as a judgment of the impact of these results or an endorsement of the process by which OpenAI obtained them. We do not speak on behalf of the entire mathematical community, and only the mathematical community can undertake the assessment that is needed.</p>
<p>Making this work public is a first step. This release is the beginning, not the completion, of the process of human understanding and the incorporation of the work into mathematical knowledge. At the same time, the future of mathematical research cannot consist only of understanding results produced by AI labs. Mathematicians must be able to formulate their own questions, develop their own approaches, and explore directions that have not been selected as examples of an AI system’s capabilities. Equitable access to powerful research tools and adequate computational resources are essential to that freedom.</p>
<p>We reaffirm our <a href="https://agmai.org/general-sep29/">published recommendations</a> on responsible release. We have discussed them with OpenAI and appreciate the company’s willingness to engage. While we consider these discussions constructive, it is ultimately up to the mathematical community to assess the extent to which our recommendations were followed successfully, and whether there are others we should suggest. We remain committed to engaging with any frontier AI lab on these questions and have already been in contact with several of them.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Another Mathematical Group Responds</h4>


<p>Some look at what is happening, and cry out ‘NO!’</p>
<p>They are going to need a much better plan than this statement.</p>
<p>Do not be fooled into thinking this group, the ‘Association for Human Mathematics,’ is a big deal. There are hundreds of thousands of mathematics PhDs, and this group has 809 members. I include it because it perfectly embodies an attitude, nothing more.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ArielKwiat/status/2108295289379668091">Ariel</a>: “Mathematicians did not ask for this work to be done” might become one of the historical quotes from this era, perfectly depicting the downfall of academia.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/emollick/status/2108248166210720107">Ethan Mollick</a>: <a href="https://ahmath.org/statements">This document</a> is going to be an assigned reading in college classes that cover this moment in time, there’s a lot happening in a few paragraphs…</p>
<p><a href="https://www.ahmath.org/statements">Association for Human Mathematicians</a>: AHM Statement on OpenAI’s October 6 Release of Mathematical Documents</p>
<p>Yesterday, on October 6th, 2026, OpenAI – which is currently defending lawsuits against accusations of illegal plagiarism, copyright infringement, and trademark dilution – released a repository of manuscripts purporting to contain solutions to a number of high-profile problems in mathematics.</p>
<p>Mathematicians did not ask for this work to be done. The Advisory Group on Mathematics and Artificial Intelligence, from whom OpenAI has claimed to derive its legitimacy, opened their initial advisory statement by saying that frontier AI corporations should not test advanced mathematical problems on internal models. In ignoring the central premise of the Advisory Group’s position, OpenAI has indicated total disregard for the norms of scientific research — norms that guarantee that mathematics remains trustworthy, ethically researched, and in the public interest.</p>
<p>Mathematicians have a particular vision of progress that is informed by history and field-specific considerations. We reject OpenAI’s assertion that this release advances our subject, and we urge mathematicians and the public to view the value of this publication model with due skepticism.</p>
<p>Releasing over 700 files at once is not a demonstration of scholarship, but a demonstration of power. We urge mathematicians to discontinue their work with OpenAI and to return to a vision of science that centers human understanding.</p>
<p><em>Association for Human Mathematics</em><br />
<em>Communications Working Group</em></p></blockquote>
<p>Whatever you think of the rest of the statement, they are not wrong that the entire exercise here was directly against what the mathematics advisory group (AGMAI) wanted. AGMAI’s central ask was that OpenAI was asked to not test hard problems on private internal models. That request, regardless of what you think of it, got fully ignored.</p>


<h4>A Different Approach</h4>


<p>OpenAI admits that ‘dump it all on GitHub’ is not exactly meeting the advisory committee’s guidelines. They are exploring better options, and are hoping in the future to also have better-written papers.</p>
<p>On top of the big drop there was also this, that came out one day earlier, where Anthropic gave their solution to mathematicians to write up before releasing it, attempting to follow the advisory group’s recommendations:</p>
<blockquote><p>New math result from Anthropic, <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06783v1">then written up by Josh Alman and Virginia Vassilevska Williams</a>, that is kind of a big deal. Anthropic had Claude research open problems, found the result, then hired Alman and Vassilevska Williams to write the paper as per what the mathematician council asked AI companies to do.</p>
<p>Abstract: We give the first polynomial improvements over the textbook algorithms for 3SUM and All-Pairs Shortest Paths (APSP): we show how to deterministically solve 3SUM on n integers of polynomial size in O(n<sup>1.9992</sup>) time and APSP on directed n-vertex graphs with polynomially bounded integer weights in O(n<sup>2.9995</sup>) time.</p>
<p>This refutes the 3SUM and APSP hypotheses. Using known reductions, we also refute the real-valued versions of the 3SUM and APSP hypotheses, the Exact Triangle hypothesis, the Zero-Weight k-Clique hypotheses, and the three rectangular hinted Online Matrix–Vector conjectures of van den Brand, Nanongkai, and Saranurak, and we give polynomial speedups for a variety of other problems.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mahdi_tcs_/status/2107308352178884924">𝖬𝖺𝗁𝖽𝗂 𝖢𝗁</a>: The what now?!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Dr_Atoosa/status/2107561210296287398">Atoosa Kasirzadeh</a>: Could you write a tweet elaborating on what this could mean from your pov?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mahdi_tcs_/status/2107568385706123723">𝖬𝖺𝗁𝖽𝗂 𝖢𝗁</a>: In a nutshell, suppose for a whole bunch of animals we knew that if they could whistle, then pigs could fly. But now someone saw a flying pig. That means we can now infer nothing about those animals (also in a sense, “dynamic programming isn’t optimal”).</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/skominers/status/2107422299821629784">Scott Kominers</a>: First off: <img alt="🤯" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/qtEgaxSFxYanT5QbJ/giffdbroph8owgkinvvj" style="height: 1em;" />. Utterly unreal result.</p>
<p>Second: I’m seeing a lot of complaints about the fact that Anthropic contracted with human experts to write up the paper – but to a first-order, isn’t this what the “Responsible Release” guidelines circulated last week say to do?</p>
<p>If Math wants to take the view that “AI labs have a responsibility to provide support, including funding, for the development of human understanding of the AI mathematical output that they release” then it’s going to have to become comfortable with these companies financing mathematicians to do just that.</p></blockquote>
<p>There was also another stray <a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2107361279254159493">new math result via GPT</a> that came out right before the massive drop of other math.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ethanepperly/status/2107331827774153038">Ethan Epperly</a>: <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06785">John Urschel just showed that the growth factor for Gaussian elimination with partial pivoting</a> is ~n^{1/2} with high probability, resolving a long-standing conjecture of Nick Trefethen!</p></blockquote>


<h4>Three Withdraws</h4>


<p><a href="https://retractionwatch.com/2026/10/08/openai-withdraws-preprints-722-manuscripts-unsolved-math-problems/">The three withdraws are all the consequences of one sign error</a>.</p>
<p>I love the idea of a website called Retraction Watch. This is one place OpenAI executed well. Mistakes happen, and even if more are found – and there will almost certainly be more errors found as the unverified 58% is still largely unvetted – the error rate is impressively low. The important thing is to own the mistakes once they are found. Retractions are often a sign you are doing something right, not something wrong.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://retractionwatch.com/2026/10/08/openai-withdraws-preprints-722-manuscripts-unsolved-math-problems/">Alicia Gallegos</a> (Retraction Watch): “I think the withdrawals and corrections will be viewed positively by the mathematics community, but it will take a lot more than that to earn back the trust they have lost,” Sutherland told us.</p></blockquote>
<p>There have also been fourteen revisions.</p>


<h4>Leaning Into Lean</h4>


<p>I liked this explanation from Jon Stokes: <a href="https://x.com/jon_stokes/status/2107820753965977620">Mathematics is great for advanced AIs, especially GPTs, because there is no ‘reward hacking.</a>’ The answer is the answer, if you do something crazy and out of distribution but it works then it works.</p>
<p>Except, are you sure? One angle is that you can either find a proof or you can find a bug in Lean, and are you sure the math will always be the easier option when you face the world’s toughest open problems?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Laneless_/status/2107671144316952906">Jai</a>: It’s just a next breakthrough predictor. A stochastic genius. Nobel autocomplete.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mroe1492/status/2107761285412618320">Michael Roe</a>: My guess is that finding exploitable soundness bugs in Lean is substantially easier than proving quasi-Riemann hypothesis. Given which, we should expect some of these proofs to be Lean exploits. Still, I’m also prepared to believe they’re real.</p>
<p>(as a computer security person, “AI can find exploitable bugs in Lean” is actually more alarming news than “AI proves the quasi-Riemann hypothesis”. Security bug apocalypse coming right up.)</p></blockquote>
<p>The bigger risk is that Lean does not confirm that the stated theorem matches up to the famous open problem. Your terms might be different, in ways that are non-obvious at first glance.</p>
<p>So far, Lean is holding up, as are all the papers with Lean proofs modulo one corrected side claim, which is rather impressive. The system works. There are 3 proofs that have broken so far, and all of them are in the unformalized 58%.</p>
<p>Of the nine results discussed up top, five are checked in Lean (quasi-RH, matrix multiplication, π, Unique Games and Hadwiger), as in the Lean-checked 42%.</p>
<p>At some point, you will give the model a task that is substantially harder than some other way to solve ‘the problem’ of you assigning the task, whether or not the alternative path involves taking over the world. Then you will be the one that has a problem.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eyqxBuxXCaamNSxBB/new-math-from-openai#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Technical people: actually consider comms](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LkheZqaMDhS8Mz4mP/technical-people-actually-consider-comms)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For years I had a blindspot in my thinking. I dismissed policy and advocacy work as “in another [lower-status] category” because I’m obviously a technical person!</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefobdvf0yg46b"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnobdvf0yg46b">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Even when I felt super pessimistic about my ability to do research that meaningfully contributes to addressing the threat of extinction from AI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m not the type of person who would be “good at comms”. I’m an autistic math guy.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7rshk3exexs"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7rshk3exexs">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Luckily comms has learnable skills, and I have gotten better through practice.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefuhd5wil4qbb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnuhd5wil4qbb">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m now prioritizing advocacy, which currently seems a lot more promising than research.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftk9wbukfzv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntk9wbukfzv">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I got lucky because, thanks to </span><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2097476196791709843"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coxon</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, AI extinction became mainstream shortly after I started. Even so, I've been very surprised to have become the biggest anti-extinction expert voice in Finland as a young person.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3gsk87zt1rj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3gsk87zt1rj">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This happened because:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have a background in AI safety research and active work affiliation with an AI safety org, showing credible expertise</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Finnish journalists want to hear from Finnish experts</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Finnish academics in media have been mostly dismissive of extinction, and</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">other genuinely concerned Finnish experts were hardly speaking up.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqfabesz3nn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqfabesz3nn">[6]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So far, most notably, I…</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Published an </span><a href="https://www.hs.fi/nakemys/art-2000012267952.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">expert op-ed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in the most prestigious slot in Finland (co-authored with </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/so8res?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@So8res</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Did four major media journalist phone interviews the following week (resulting in </span><a href="https://www.is.fi/digitoday/tietoturva/art-2000012287673.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this article</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.is.fi/digitoday/art-2000012296388.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this follow-up</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsckthpbszsb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsckthpbszsb">[7]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Had a </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MAgWz3nmG7M"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">podcast</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> interview (totalling tens of thousands of views), and was asked for a profile in a top-tier news outlet, which I declined.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffvfxamzys3u"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfvfxamzys3u">[8]</a></sup></span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Got meetings with politicians, including some important ones.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My message is that human extinction is very likely if superintelligence is developed on the current trajectory, and I want Finland to push for an international prohibition on its development.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some technical people doing comms have had a big impact: e.g. Hinton, Bengio, Yudkosky, Soares. Samuel Buteau single-handedly made the Canadian cross-party ControlAI </span><a href="https://controlai.org/canada-statement/en" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">statement</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> happen. But you don't need to be as high-status as them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I've had a surprising amount of impact in speaking out as someone in AI safety research.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpj8moorb06"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpj8moorb06">[9]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you think you're not the type of person for comms, please actually consider it.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnobdvf0yg46b"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefobdvf0yg46b">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think a lot of people motivated to help with the extinction threat also dismiss comms without good reasons. I was motivated to write this post because of, for example, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HekpnSkrt89tMm3Dc/i-am-an-ai-safety-researcher"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">these</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FogmcDHA6AdMGukum/alignment-engineering-vs-misalignment-science#fn1dhnvsy3479j"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">posts</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that discuss prioritization (when it's difficult to have positive impact in technical AI safety research) and use an appendix/footnote to scope out discussion of comms. If it's difficult to have positive impact in technical AI safety, speaking out / doing advocacy is often a much better way to have impact.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7rshk3exexs"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7rshk3exexs">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I graduated undergrad in math and computer science in one year because math was the easiest subject. I've had a ~technical AI safety research career for ~3 years.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnuhd5wil4qbb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefuhd5wil4qbb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thank you </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nKsyMfNsAuTrxmjmi/quick-notes-from-teaching-technical-profiles-how-to-talk-in"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Camille</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and others who have helped with my practice. I did media training sessions with different coaches and practiced with ChatGPT voice mode, 30 min – 3 h every day over a couple of weeks. Thank you Eliezer and Nate for the advice on communicating honestly and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CYTwRZtrhHuYf7QYu/a-case-for-courage-when-speaking-of-ai-danger" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">transparently</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> instead of only strategically.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntk9wbukfzv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftk9wbukfzv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems to me that we definitely need more time and are not on track to solve alignment even with more investment. I imagine a lot of AI safety researchers also think we need more time.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3gsk87zt1rj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3gsk87zt1rj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Not because I’m young. They contacted me because I have credible expertise and I disagree with the academics and take extinction seriously compared to other Finnish experts available for comment. (They found out about me through my op-ed.) </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqfabesz3nn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqfabesz3nn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I did talk to some other concerned experts, and having heard their stated reasons not to, I feel confident that speaking up is good in expectation.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsckthpbszsb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsckthpbszsb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the article, I gave a brief scenario ending with superintelligence boiling the oceans. There were some tweets about how implausible this is; </span><a href="https://x.com/LNummenmaa/status/2102643607933259975?s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">one</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with 100k+ views criticizing the newspaper for failing to “at least superficially” fact-check the claims, </span><a href="https://x.com/tanhisjussi/status/2102489731036516710?s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">another</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> calling me a “DEI hire”, and another saying I should become a housewife. A Finnish-language tweet with 100k+ views is roughly as rare as 10M+ views on an English-language tweet, based on adjusting down from Claude estimates.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfvfxamzys3u"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffvfxamzys3u">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I declined because I didn’t expect fair coverage.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpj8moorb06"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpj8moorb06">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think non-technical people can have big impact through comms, especially talking to politicians, see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pbKrZCzhsnar6iaAH/how-a-cold-email-got-the-finnish-government-to-respond-on" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How a cold email got the Finnish government to respond on superintelligence regulation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Following </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HekpnSkrt89tMm3Dc/i-am-an-ai-safety-researcher" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FogmcDHA6AdMGukum/alignment-engineering-vs-misalignment-science" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">tradition</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (see footnote 1), I'll say in a footnote that this post is aimed at people in AI safety research. Because the post is based on my experience as a technical person. :P</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LkheZqaMDhS8Mz4mP/technical-people-actually-consider-comms#comments">Discuss</a>

### [A critical look at Lean4](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zHhGa3iQBQcdSifFL/a-critical-look-at-lean4-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lots of the discussion around Lean certificates and trust centers around general philosophical ideas of how much humans should trust formal certificates. It's important to remember, though, that Lean4 is not an ideal formal certificate machine: Lean4 is a software in its infancy and has issues. There are reasonable reasons to specifically not trust Lean4.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we're going to be putting our society's trust in Lean4, I think that it's good to have an open critical discussion of how warranted that trust is.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epistemic status: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most of this work is a factual description about aspects of Lean4 and set theory. I am 85% sure that at most two of these factual descriptions are incorrect. I am 90% sure that, in the current version of Lean4 on Github, there exists a proof of the statement "True=False" that would be accepted in such a way that the Lean community would call it a bug in need of patching (either by messing with the elaborator, compiler, kernel, or some other part of the Lean). I am 2% certain that we will soon be putting huge amounts of trust into Lean4 certificates produced by misaligned AIs, and that the human work we do as a community thinking about and fixing Lean4 in the next 24 months would fall under Less Wrong's classification of a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/pivotal-act" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pivotal act</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The current status of things</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's a quote from a </span><a href="https://leodemoura.github.io/blog/2026-4-2-why-lean/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">last April </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">be Leo de Moura, the creator of Lean.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 76.66989237160121%;"><img alt="leo-de-moura.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791519691/lexical_client_uploads/f9invokjx7ggtmppvbpb.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's </span><a href="https://leodemoura.github.io/blog/2026-8-1-postmortem-for-kernel-soundness-bug-14576/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">another post by Leo de Moura</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, three months later:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 78.85196374622356%;"><img alt="leo-de-moura2.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791520007/lexical_client_uploads/yopbf9ut7zorycs7u45b.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Following this incident, the Lean FRO found </span><a href="https://leodemoura.github.io/blog/2026-8-24-postmortem-for-the-kernel-soundness-bug-hunt/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">seven more bugs in the kernel</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The new opinion of Leo de Moura: "[False statements being accepted by Lean] is going to keep happening. AIs are really good at exploiting soundness bugs in the kernels".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What was the Lean community's reaction to "soundness bug #14576"? There's a nice thread about it on a Lean developer's forum </span><a href="https://leanprover.zulipchat.com/#narrow/channel/270676-lean4/topic/Counterexample.20to.20the.20Lean.20Conjecture.20.28Soundness.20Bug.29" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><img alt="Screenshot 2026-10-08 at 9.33.12 PM.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791520397/lexical_client_uploads/vzplii50m6hbezmdubey.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Uh oh! Not only do we expect bugs, but there are possibilities of "sea monsters". This article is a bit about the bugs, a bit about the sea monsters, and a bit about the unsafe ways that Lean4 is currently being used (e.g. the un-sandboxed comparator used in </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/formalizing-fermats-last-theorem" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic's formalization of Fermat's Last Theorem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) .</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What does it mean for Lean4 to be consistent?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lean4 lets you write mathematical statements and proofs using the bare low-level primitive notions of mathematics, and checks that the proofs are valid using the fundamental axioms. Which axioms? The most standard mathematical axioms are the "Zermelo-Fraenkel plus Choice" axioms (ZFC). These axioms have been vetted by armies of nerds and we're really quite certain that they are consistent. Lean4 is not based on ZFC. It is based on a special home-brew version of dependent type theory, which was designed by picking and choosing features of various modern type theories, with the governing design principle that it should be fast at compile time.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How do we know that Lean4's type theory is consistent? Ideally we would construct some totally convincing proof that if ZFC (or some standard strengthening of of it via </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_cardinal" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">large cardinal axioms</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) is consistent, then Lean4 is consistent. However, we currently don't know how to do this</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As of writing, there is no publicly available proof that Lean4 is consistent with respect to ZFC + any large cardinal axiom.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When I originally learned that Lean4 had no known consistency proof, I thought this was some sort of bug or technicality. Surely we understand why Lean4 is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">supposed </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to be consistent, we just haven't bothered writing out the details? My current read of the situation is that this is not the case. Lean4 is not known to consistent, we don't know why it should be consistent, and there are real obstructions to all known proof strategies for showing that Lean4 is consistent.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Smart people make fundamentally inconsistent type theories all the time</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lean4 is a "dependent type theory". Dependent type theory as a foundation for mathematics was pioneered in in 1971 by </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intuitionistic_type_theory" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Martin-Löf</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. A year after Martin-Löf came out with his type theory, Girard found a contradiction in Martin-Löf's theory. In his </span><a href="https://archive-pml.github.io/martin-lof/pdfs/An-Intuitionistic-Theory-of-Types-1972.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">revised paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Martin-Löf concluded that one of the axioms “had to be abandoned” and describes the necessary change as “so drastic” that the theory lost substantial expressive strength. Oops! Constructing good dependent type theories is hard.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Outside the world of dependent type theory there are other famous examples of mathematicians trying to come up with new foundations of mathematics and making deep, subtle, fundamental mistakes: Church's 1932 proposed foundations for mathematics using lambda calculus (which was shown to be inconsistent by </span><a href="https://www.cis.upenn.edu/~cis5110/Kleene-InconsistencyCertainFormal-1935.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kleene and Rosser</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), and Quine's 1940 proposed reworking of ZFC (which was shown to be inconsistent by </span><a href="https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/journal-of-symbolic-logic/article/abs/buraliforti-paradox/01B4F184BDBDB8F5C29A2D10ECF83907" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rosser</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The status of Lean4's consistency proof</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coming up with consistent foundations for math is tricky business. The ingredients that make Lean4 fast at compile time are the same ingredients give it access to an unusually strong amount of recursion. For instance, a common proof strategy for showing that dependent type theories are consistent with respect to ZFC + large cardinals is to use the so called "normalization" property. It was proved in 2019 by Abel and Coquand that this </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1911.08174v4" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">normalization property fails for type theories like Lean's</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which in some precise sense means that Lean4's type theory is wild/poorly behaved.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There was a proof of Lean3's consistency </span><a href="https://github.com/digama0/lean-type-theory/releases/tag/v1.0" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">proposed in 2019</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by Mario Carneiro. This proof works by embedding the primitive notions of Lean3 into the primitive notions of set theory (sets, functions between them), and then showing that the axioms of Lean3 correspond to true statements about sets. Proving that type theories are consistent with respect to ZFC + large cardinals via embedding into set theory is standard business. Unfortunately it came out in 2024 that </span><a href="https://hottest-seminar.github.io/hottestfiles/Carneiro-2025-02-06-HoTTEST.pdf#page=52" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">there was a flaw in his proof strategy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (oops!) and that the correct proof would require a new approach. Even if the Lean3 embedding gets fixed, Lean4 adds new features which require non-trivial extensions of that embedding.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another sign that Lean4's type theory is complicated/wild is the recent </span><a href="https://Joachim Breitner" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">con-leche</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> project, which proposes a set-theory embedding that captures a subset of the properties of Lean4. In contrast to Mario Carneiro's Lean3 proof (which used a relatively tame large cardinal axiom, "ZFC_omega"), the </span><a href="https://Joachim Breitner" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">con-leche</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> project uses arbitrarily long nested families of Grothendieck universes. This is a strong assumption.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I recently asked my GPT-6 Astra to find an embedding of Lean4 into set theory. It spent ~25h and several thousand dollars on the task, and it did not succeed. It kept trying new embeddings and tricks, but every time it found counterexamples using the strong recursive power of Lean4. So it seems to me like finding this set theory embedding is a genuinely non-trivial task, and we don't have a high-level picture of why Lean4 should be consistent.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Untamed recursion is bug-prone</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of the main things which makes Lean4 logically complex are its "nested inductive types", a sort of declaration where you can define a type in terms of a higher-order property of its own type. Nested inductive types are a new research area, but our understanding is still in its infancy (we've had some good progress in 2026, </span><a href="https://hal.science/hal-05366368v2/document" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2306.10124" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Preliminary evidence suggests they might be pretty wild. Here's a quote from the former of those two papers:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Considering the importance of nested inductive types, one would expect Rocq and Lean to offer good support for them, yet neither one does. They both fail to generate usable elimination principles, and surprisingly accept a different set of nested inductive types, neither of which is satisfactory...</span><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[technical paragraph about a problem with Lean's implementation of nested inductive types]</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">... this workaround highlights the need for a more systematic treatment of the problem—one that avoids such leakage of the internal encoding. More problematic, since a straightforward mutual encoding becomes impractical in more complex scenarios, Lean ultimately rejects some nested definitions that are theoretically valid—and often crucial for advanced specifications.</span><br /><br /><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These implementation shortcomings likely stem from an incomplete foundational understanding of nested inductive types.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The conclusion from the literature, in my view, is that even if Lean4's type theory is consistent at a high level, the nature of nested inductive types makes Lean4's recursion wild. That behavior could be so wild that it is fundamentally incompatible with logic (i.e. is inconsistent), but even assuming that's not the case, it could easily be wild enough to cause bugs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is what Chris Bailey was referring to as the "sea monsters" from before - aspects of nested inductive types which are consistent, but wild and ill-behaved and cause bugs. It's impossible to do a systematic search for this bugs currently, because we don't know at a high level what we're looking for. There's no spec to track because nobody knows how Lean4's type theory is supposed to behave.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"But doesn't con-leche give a proof?"</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There was recently a project announced called </span><a href="https://github.com/leanprover/con-leche" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">con-leche</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which claims to give a formally-verified Lean4 kernel written in Lean4. This is the second attempt at such a project, the first of which is called </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.14064" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lean4lean</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and is still under development.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"[Con-leche] intentionally explores a different point in the design space than </span><a href="https://github.com/digama0/lean4lean/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lean4lean</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: We compromise on the checker implementation (annotations, extra checks, generated inductive models) so that we can have a direct model-based proof of consistency that does not need some of the hard-to-prove metatheoretical properties of Lean" - Joachim Breitner, creator of con-leche</span></p></blockquote><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Con-leche does not claim to say anything about whether official Lean4 C++ kernel is consistent. It construct its own kernel, and claims that the kernel it constructs is consistent. Con-leche and the official Lean4 handle nested inductive types differently. There are statements which are accepted by the official Lean4 kernel but rejected by con-leche.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I tried using Astra to translate con-leche's proof into a proof of a proof of the consistency of the official Lean4 C++ kernel, and there was a real failure in the proof translation.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The changes con-leche makes are specifically to avoid the sea monsters. The fact that this embedding works for con-leche does not resolve the relevant issues that make Lean4 difficult.</span><br /></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is really subtle set theory and logic afoot. In principle, for the obvious reason, using con-leche to prove the consistency of con-leche is circular. What you try to do is only use the model of set theory embedded within con-leche to prove that con-leche is consistent. However, you have to be careful that your model of set theory hasn't been corrupted by the inconsistent theory surrounding it.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The con-leche experiment is nice (it's good to have more kernels!), but doesn't solve the "sea monster" questions, and it doesn't rule out bugs in the official kernel.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefela1m69m4ln"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnela1m69m4ln">[1]</a></sup></span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Using Lean4 safely</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here is the breakdown of the compute cost associated to running </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/formalizing-fermats-last-theorem" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic's  Lean4 certificate</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of Fermat's Last Theorem:</span></p><img alt="Screenshot 2026-10-07 at 1.19.22 PM.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791527799/lexical_client_uploads/pi9ncg4jqqxwbqhwazhf.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is this "comparator" thing that takes all the runtime? The comparator is an extra tool which is useful for making sure that malicious code in the proof body does not mess up the theorem statement during the elaboration+compilation process. This is something you have to worry about even if you trust the kernel completely. As you can see, it takes a long time to run.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Soon, we expect that the world's critical software infrastructure will be formally verified. This is a huge project, which is quite expensive. The difference between running basic Lean4 and running Lean4 + comparator could be the difference between "expensive but doable" and prohibitive.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The broader point is that there are choices  you make when you use Lean4. If you wanted, you could run your code in con-leche and nanoda. You could use the comparator. You could sandbox your cached .olean files. None of these are the default behavior of Lean4; these are choices you have to opt into as a developer. The right choice surely depends on the application, and your level of trust in both in Lean4 and agent swarms.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How much do we trust the agents?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There's an aspect of Anthropic's FLT certificate I find worrying. When running the comparator, they decided to explicitly disable the sandbox that separates the untrusted proof code from the trusted statement. In their codebase, when justifying their decision, they </span><a href="https://github.com/anthropics/fermats-last-theorem/blob/main/verification/comparator/run.sh" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">make the following comment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Comparator's own scripts/fake-landrun.sh is used = no sandbox, which is how the recorded run was made; </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the sandbox guards against an untrusted solution, which is moot when you built the tree yourself</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">."</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems like Anthropic decided that the agent swarm that wrote the FLT proof is trusted, and that guarding against malicious code (even when it is easy to do) is "moot". Do we as a community think this is okay? FLT was a landmark formalization. I can easily imagine somebody pointing to the FLT certificate and saying "of course you should trust my certificate, it passed the level of rigor that FLT did". I think we should set higher standards.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjq5c74yzlwd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjq5c74yzlwd">[2]</a></sup></span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Where do we go from here?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All of the issues I talked about seem temporary or fixable. Assuming that Lean4 is consistent, then a proof should exist and we should be able to find it. We should be able to use that understanding of Lean4's foundations to run a systematic bug-search through the Lean4 kernel. We should be able to change the default "maximum trust" behavior of Lean4 to involve significant sandboxing, with formally-proven specs showing that untrusted proofs aren't able to tamper with trusted theorem statements. We should, we should, we should.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unfortunately, even though Lean4 is open source, not everybody is allowed to participate in this journey. Fixing Lean4's safety issues counts as a cybersecurity task, and as such (in my experience), Astra will refrain from answering questions. Astra was happy to try to prove that Lean4 was consistent, but when I asked it to take seriously the possibility that Lean4 is not consistent it shut down the chat as cybersecurity. When I ran Anthropic's FLT certificate on my computer, I asked Astra to take a critical look at the logs to make sure there was no funny business and it classified that chat as cybersecurity.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We're entering a world where cybersecurity is a task reserved for those that frontier labs deem worthy. This essay is in part a letter for them: please take Lean4's correctness seriously.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lean4 is in its infancy. There are still significant hurdles between where we are now and where it needs to be before we can confidently expect it to hold the weight of the world's critical infrastructure against cyberattacks by sophistical, lazy, reward-hacking AI agents.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnela1m69m4ln"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefela1m69m4ln">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On a more personal note, related to the con-leche experiment: I hope we don't end up in a future where the most trusted software in the world ends up being in a situation where we are told to trust it because of an AI-generated certificate, especially one that has room for loopholes. Certificates are not replacements for understanding.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjq5c74yzlwd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjq5c74yzlwd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I ran the comparator with the sandbox on the FLT certificate and it checked out. Kevin Buzzard also independently checked the FLT certificate and ran various due-diligence checks. The real community verification of the FLT certificate was at a higher standard than Anthropic's repo.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zHhGa3iQBQcdSifFL/a-critical-look-at-lean4-1#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Stop telling people to pivot to AI safety, I beg you](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xLgJJC3CD3y3AhtKn/stop-telling-people-to-pivot-to-ai-safety-i-beg-you)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epistemic status: Feeling frustrated, and therefore somewhat uncharitable.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> very frustrated with calls for people to “</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">drop everything and pivot into AI safety</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">like</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://ceselder.substack.com/p/pivot-to-ai-safety-i-beg-you"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">t</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">his one</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. (See also </span><a href="https://benjamintodd.substack.com/p/work-on-ai-risk" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://80000hours.org/ai/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QCrT2DTJNfvuqtppB/ai-safety-s-biggest-talent-gap-isn-t-researchers-it-s" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://80000hours.substack.com/p/how-to-pivot-to-ai-safety-without" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for recent calls for more people to go into AI safety; though my frustration was triggered by the Celeste's post.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are far more talented people trying to get into AI safety than there are jobs for them.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmv0lzsipsz"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmv0lzsipsz">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/vvocfhQ7bcBR4FLBx/apply-to-the-second-ml-for-alignment-bootcamp-mlab-2-in"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MLAB 2</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which I did in 2022, was already competitive to get into, at about 5% acceptance rate (admitted about 40 out of 800 applicants). Things are much, much more competitive now. There is a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PiP4JqQFKhoqHGG2n/how-to-apply-to-ai-safety-fellowships-and-beyond#The_AI_Safety_fellowship_pipeline"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">whole pipeline of AI safety fellowships</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (and guides for applying to them). It is rumored that one recent cohort of the Anthropic Fellows program had over </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">10,000</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> applicants. (Anecdotally, I applied to the Anthropic Fellows program in early 2026, after working at Redwood, METR, and an ML startup, and I didn’t get in!)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And, uh, just take a look at the AI safety jobs? The bottom of the salary range for METR’s current “Member of Technical Staff, Embedded Assessments” </span><a href="https://jobs.lever.co/metr/69792e29-e72c-44a2-8305-82cb40ab17c5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">job posting</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is $402k. This is higher than the median total comp for Research Scientists (</span><a href="https://www.levels.fyi/t/software-engineer/title/research-scientist?country=254"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">$310k</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) and Machine Learning Engineers (</span><a href="https://www.levels.fyi/t/software-engineer/title/machine-learning-engineer?country=254"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">$282k</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Plus these jobs are high status too. These are really good jobs and nobody needs to be convinced to want them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think it’s Actually Bad to keep saying AI safety is </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">talent-constrained</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I think it really harms people to tell them they should drop what they’re doing to switch to AI safety when there isn’t a path for the vast majority of the people who do to thrive.
