These are good, talented people who just want to help, and they could be doing </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lots of other good things</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> instead of putting themselves </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">through the meatgrinder of applying to AI safety jobs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Finally, I worry that believing that the field is talent-constrained is holding us back as a field. If we believed that we just needed to wait for the right talent to come along, then we can stop looking elsewhere and fixing fixable problems now. I worry that the “AI safety industrial complex” has in fact gotten too effective at getting new, amazing talent into the field, so that the existing orgs can afford to continue to churn through them without becoming better. </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If a regular startup treated talent the same way as AI safety orgs, it would fail very quickly and be forgotten</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. No, you do not keep saying to the world that your field is “talent-constrained” while doing “field building” at top universities. </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You get to work with your existing team and build the damn thing.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmv0lzsipsz"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmv0lzsipsz">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My own experience is with the technical side of things, but I </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/ZCqouBodPxLdgfMxA/jenba-s-quick-takes?commentId=N8MDMgybjcfWGDvj5" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hear</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that the situation is not that different on the generalist/operator side.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xLgJJC3CD3y3AhtKn/stop-telling-people-to-pivot-to-ai-safety-i-beg-you#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Social Theory and Agent Foundations](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DwHvtm8vLipAQPest/social-theory-and-agent-foundations)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lately I’ve been </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/contra-caplan-on-higher-education"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">writing a lot</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/higher-education-as-class-commitment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the sociopolitical considerations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/economic-efficiency-often-undermines"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">economic-style reasoning misses</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I view this work as continuous with my agent foundations research; in this post, I lay out some of the connections I see between sociology and agent foundations. I’ll particularly focus on attempts to pin down theories of rational social and political behavior, as an extension to (or replacement of) the theory of rational economic behavior originally laid out by Von Neumann and Morgenstern.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post will of course be far from exhaustive. However, it’s worth noting that attempts to develop big-picture frameworks for social theory have grown surprisingly sparse. Berger describes the field as being </span><a href="https://firstthings.com/whatever-happened-to-sociology/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">deformed from two directions</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> since the mid-1900s: on one side, by the “methodological fetishism” of “sociologists using increasingly sophisticated methods to study increasingly trivial topics”; and on the other side, by the “cultural revolution [that] sought to transform sociology from a science into an instrument of ideological advocacy”. Hence many of the thinkers I’ll be discussing were officially based in economics or political science departments.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post has three sections, each focused on a different topic: social and game-theoretic equilibria; the internalization of norms and values; and collective behavior. In a follow-up post, I’ll talk about more mainstream sociological preoccupations of capitalism and class, and how they relate to rationalist thinking.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Social equilibria vs game-theoretic equilibria</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One important concept is that of a “social equilibrium”: a social system which, when disturbed, would tend to return to its original state. This concept was pioneered by Vilfredo Pareto in the early 1900s, and further developed by Talcott Parsons in the mid-1900s. While I haven’t investigated Parsons deeply, his work seems important—Herbert Gintis claimed (in 2009) that “sociological theory has atrophied since the death of Talcott Parsons in 1979”.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref46jc3r0zq6c"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn46jc3r0zq6c">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, progress towards a formal definition of social equilibria has been slow, because it’s hard to model disturbances to a game-theoretic equilibrium. The concepts of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trembling_hand_perfect_equilibrium"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">trembling hand equilibria</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionarily_stable_strategy"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">evolutionarily stable strategies</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stochastically_stable_equilibrium"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">stochastically stable equilibria</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are three attempts to describe more robust versions of Nash equilibria. However, they only model arbitrary or random deviations from equilibria, rather than ones deliberately caused by players themselves. This leads to problems when talking about counterfactual behavior, as </span><a href="https://ideas.repec.org/p/els/esrcls/047.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kenneth Binmore articulated in 1997</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What keeps a rational player on the equilibrium path is his evaluation of what would happen if he were to deviate. But, if he were to deviate, he would behave irrationally. Other players would then be foolish if they were not to take this evidence of irrationality into account in planning their responses to the deviation. A formal model that neglects what would happen if a rational player were to deviate from rational play must therefore be missing something important, no matter how elaborately it is analyzed. However, Aumann, for example, is insistent that his conclusions say nothing whatever about what players would do if vertices of the game tree off the backward-induction path were to be reached. But, if nothing can be said about what would happen off the backward-induction path, then it seems obvious that nothing can be said about the rationality of remaining on the backward-induction path.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In his 2009 book The Bounds of Reason, Herbert Gintis claims that the problem comes from the common knowledge of rationality criterion, arguing that backward induction gives counterintuitive results because common knowledge of rationality is too strong an assumption.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreff7lcmbd4g7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnf7lcmbd4g7">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> There is a literature on forward induction which aims to address the same problem Binmore describes, but which seem to rely on similarly strong criteria, such as </span><a href="https://faculty.wcas.northwestern.edu/msi661/bs01fwd.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">rationality and common strong belief of rationality</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These assumptions, as well as the concept of Nash equilibria in general, seem to me to be hacks to get around the core sociological problem that Parsons called “double contingency”: the idea that communication (and strategic interaction more generally) requires people to model another person who is simultaneously modeling them back. Intuitively speaking, double contingency is the reason why we can’t talk about how rational agents converge to a game-theoretic equilibrium (or which one they converge to), only what happens when they’re already at an equilibrium. Two other prominent explorations of this same core idea are </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keynesian_beauty_contest"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Keynesian beauty contests</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (1936) and </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Focal_point_(game_theory)"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Schelling points</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (1960) (see also Scott Alexander </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Kbm6QnJv9dgWsPHQP/schelling-fences-on-slippery-slopes"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/lifeboat-games-and-backscratchers"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and my discussion of </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/i/213791120/languages-as-schelling-points"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">languages as Schelling points</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Harsanyi identified a very similar issue in </span><a href="https://gamef21.classes.ryansafner.com/readings/Harsanyi-1967.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a 1967 paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems to me that the basic reason why the theory of games with incomplete information has made so little progress so far lies in the fact that these games give rise, or at least appear to give rise, to an infinite regress in reciprocal expectations on the part of the players.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Harsanyi’s response was to develop </span><a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/nonwellfounded-set-theory/harsanyi-type-spaces.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a notion of types</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> which defined players’ beliefs about the whole infinite regress. Meanwhile </span><a href="http://www.ma.huji.ac.il/~raumann/pdf/Agreeing%20to%20Disagree.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aumann’s approach</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (in 1976) was to formalize the notion of common knowledge, which allowed him to prove </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aumann%27s_agreement_theorem"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">his agreement theorem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. And Gintis argued that we should interpret social norms not as Nash equilibria but rather as </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Correlated_equilibrium"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">correlated equilibria</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (in which a “choreographer” sends a signal before the game which agents can use to coordinate). I haven’t investigated any of these approaches deeply enough to confidently evaluate them, but none pattern-match to the kind of insight which might make us much less confused about double contingency. Even </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1609.05058"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Leike et al.’s solution to the grain of truth problem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> required </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1508.04145"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reflective oracles</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that could be accessed by all players.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead, I’m more excited about research on </span><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20161221061828/https://acritch.com/osgt-is-weird/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lobian cooperation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which provides an interesting and counterintuitive model of agents cutting through the infinite recursion of modeling each other. It was </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TNfx89dh5KkcKrvho/ai-cooperation-in-practice" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">first discovered by Slepnev</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, written up by </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1401.5577"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Barasz et al.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1602.04184"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">extended by Critch</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In its original form, it only applied to proof-based agents who are given each other’s source code. However, we’d ideally like a version of it applicable to agents with probabilistic beliefs about the world and each other (like </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/y5GftLezdozEHdXkL/an-intuitive-guide-to-garrabrant-induction"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Garrabrant inductors</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Some work that moves in this direction has been done by </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iCDBQtby4L2fZ7yns/payor-s-lemma-in-natural-language"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Payor</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2WpPRrqrFQa6n2x3W/modal-fixpoint-cooperation-without-loeb-s-theorem"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Critch</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZWhJcHPmRaXAPAK5k/probabilistic-payor-lemma"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Demski</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My longer-term hope is that work in this direction helps build up a formal understanding of the kind of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intersubjectivity"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">intersubjective</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> social epistemology that Gintis calls for in the quote below:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The most fundamental failure of game theory is its lack of a theory of when and how rational agents share mental constructs. The Bayesian rational actors favored by contemporary game theory live in a universe of subjectivity and instead of constructing a truly social epistemology, game theorists have developed a variety of subterfuges that make it appear that rational agents may enjoy a commonality of belief (common priors, common knowledge), but all are failures.</span></p></blockquote><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Internalizing norms</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Parsons took a different approach to the problem of double contingency, by focusing on agents which </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">internalize</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> norms and values. He discusses this process in </span><a href="https://tomek.org/files/the_social_system.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">his 1951 book The Social System</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and argues that it’s necessary to hold together social equilibria:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is only by virtue of internalization or institutionalized values that a genuine motivational integration of behavior in the social structure takes place, that the “deeper” layers of motivation become harnessed to the fulfillment of role-expectations. It is only when this has taken place to a high degree that it is possible to say that a social system is highly integrated, and that the interests of the collectivity and the private interests of its constituent members can be said to approach coincidence.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This integration of a set of common value patterns with the internalized need-disposition structure of the constituent personalities is the core phenomenon of the dynamics of social systems. That the stability of any social system except the most evanescent interaction process is dependent on a degree of such integration may be said to be the fundamental dynamic theorem of sociology. It is the major point of reference for all analysis which may claim to be a dynamic analysis of social process.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is the significance of institutional integration in this sense which lies at the basis of the place of specifically sociological theory in the sciences of action and the reasons why economic theory and other versions of the conceptual schemes which give predominance to rational instrumental goal-orientation cannot provide an adequate model for the dynamic analysis of the social system in general terms. It has been repeatedly shown that reduction of motivational dynamics to rational instrumental terms leads straight to the Hobbesian thesis, which is a reductio ad absurdum of the concept of a social system. This reductio was carried out in classic form by Durkheim in his Division of Labor. But Durkheim’s excellent functional analysis has since been enormously reinforced by the implications of modern psychological knowledge with reference to the conditions of socialization and the bases of psychological security and the stability of personality, as well as much further empirical and theoretical analysis of social systems as such.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think that Parsons concedes too much here. Even if internalization of norms is an important driver of social behavior, it’s still possible in principle for that to coexist with rational goal-orientation. Indeed, much work in AI alignment has been motivated by the idea of agents rationally modifying themselves, including by making commitments over time. On the other hand, value change has always been hard to reason about in economic frameworks, so perhaps it was reasonable for Parsons to interpret norm internalization as being opposed to (the dominant conception of) rationality.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, my sense is that the econ-minded parts of the field went too far in the other direction over the following decades—as I’ll illustrate with James Coleman’s 1990 book </span><a href="https://www.hup.harvard.edu/books/9780674312265"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Foundations of Social Theory</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It starts with a declaration that he’ll interpret humans as utility maximizers, and follows with 1000 pages of analysis of social phenomena from that lens. In doing so, Coleman showcases the limitations of economic-style thinking about social behavior. For example, his characterization of norms involves individuals holding the right to control decisions about others’ actions. However, in doing so he appeals to implicit property rights over decisions, punting our confusion about norms to the level of how such rights are created and maintained.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s been some notable work since Coleman. In particular, Binmore argued that norms should be viewed as equilibrium-selection devices (building on the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/d2HvpKWQ2XGNsHr8s/hell-is-game-theory-folk-theorems"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">game theory folk theorem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which established that almost any outcome can be a Nash equilibrium in indefinitely repeated games). Claude also pointed me to Bicchieri’s characterization of norms as conditional preferences and Skyrms’ characterization of signaling conventions. There’s also a bunch of work about norm-following as reputation management; the paper that I’m most familiar with is Paul de Font-Reaulx’s </span><a href="https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/phpr.70064"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do expected utility maximizers have commitment issues?</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I haven’t explored any of these very deeply, but my sense is that all of these approaches either restrict how agents reason about each other or take shared expectations as given.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m more interested in research which tries to “get underneath” the standard assumptions of game theory. Hence I’ll return to Coleman—while most of his book builds on existing frameworks, he does question them in Chapter 19, on the construction of the self. Coleman identifies that the standard assumption of a unitary self is suspicious. One alternative he proposes is to distinguish between the “object self” (which has interests and preferences) and the “acting self” (which makes decisions on behalf of the object self). Outside the context of the individual, Coleman describes this as similar to the relationships between children and parents, or shareholders and managers. In the AI context, this is similar to the terminal vs instrumental goal distinction; and also to the relationships between humans and corrigible AIs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, I think that progress towards a theory of sociopolitical rationality </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FuGfR3jL3sw6r8kB4/richard-ngo-s-shortform?commentId=EHhfmt37ZnNeLRP4P"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">will require moving beyond this distinction</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. An approach I like more is to think of agents as coalitions of subagents; Coleman’s term for this is “corporate actors” (by which he means not just corporations but any system that “incorporates” many actors). </span><a href="https://www.picoeconomics.org/Nut2/Nutshell2.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ainslie’s picoeconomics</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> takes a similar approach. A major preoccupation of such coalitional agents is resolving internal inconsistencies; Coleman mentions </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balance_theory"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Heider’s “balance theory” of psychology</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://www.amazon.com/dp/B0DD4CPZJY?lv=shuf&amp;channelId=500&amp;plpRedirect=mhFallback"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lecky’s self-consistency theory of personality</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, as both focusing on this. I also characterized identity shifts in terms of people “working to find or maintain [consistent] internal narratives” in my post </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/higher-education-as-class-commitment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Higher Education as Class Commitment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One difficulty in developing these models is that, until recently, we didn’t have many formal frameworks for describing internal inconsistency. Richardson’s </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.11862"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">probabilistic dependency graphs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/y5GftLezdozEHdXkL"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Garrabrant’s inductors</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/e7Pd4Q9TF7jFdmPgz/imprecise-beliefs-a-tiny-introduction"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Davidad’s imprecise beliefs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, are three recent frameworks for doing so. However, my sense is that they still don’t capture important aspects of internal conflict—e.g. dynamics where individuals try to block out information, which seem important for explaining how egos and identities work. To do so, I expect we’ll need to move beyond the Bayesian expected utility maximization paradigm, towards </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/explaining-knightianism-on-one-foot"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">theories of how to act rationally under Knightian uncertainty</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefy665y8b63r9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fny665y8b63r9">[3]</a></sup></span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Collective behavior</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The frameworks for internal inconsistency I mentioned above still focus on the single-agent setting, despite value change typically being induced by multi-agent interactions. My sense is that the best thinking on this topic is mostly non-formal, and weaves together insights from a variety of domains—like </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimetic_theory"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Girard on mimesis</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://benjaminrosshoffman.com/guilt-shame-and-depravity/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hoffman on depravity</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://hazardoustimes.substack.com/p/towards-a-unified-theory-of-self"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Spence on self-deception</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and Lacan on desire.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7wid7wypjso"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7wid7wypjso">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And of course we can trace related ideas further back to Freud and Jung. Unfortunately, I’m not aware of many people deeply familiar with continental thought who are writing with the kind of clarity I typically look for (the writers of </span><a href="https://suspendedreason.com/2021/05/11/pfeilstorch-progress/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pfeilstorch</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> being a notable exception). I’ve personally tried to link some of these ideas to the game theory framework </span><a href="https://postagi.org/talks/ngo-self-destruction"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in this talk</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/higher-education-as-class-commitment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To discuss the standard game-theoretic perspective on collective behavior, I’ll start with the other chapter from Foundations of Social Theory I found most interesting: Chapter 9, on collective behavior. One thing I like is that Coleman’s central examples of game-theoretic interactions are more realistic than most thought experiments. Rather than a Prisoner’s Dilemma he talks about the “game” of leaving a theater after someone yells fire. The best outcome is everyone walking calmly out; the worst outcome is a stampede. This provides a visceral intuition for how cooperation can be a flexible and continuous process (as I discuss in </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/ai-as-orderly-evacuation-vs-stampede"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coleman understands the intuition that there’s something irrational about the defect-defect equilibrium. His solution (reminiscent of his definition of norms discussed above) is to talk about individuals transferring control of their actions to others. This lets him describe a coordination mechanism similar to FDT-style correlations (although Coleman explicitly denies that his framework is relevant to single-shot games). Though his mathematical framework doesn’t look particularly promising, one thing I like about this approach is that the idea of transferring control to others makes correlated behavior something which agents </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">decide</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to induce, whereas FDT-style correlations are taken as given. So Coleman’s approach is in some ways closer to </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/i/213791120/actions-and-entanglements"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">my own nascent notion of “entanglement”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Elsewhere in his book Coleman also discusses an earlier cluster of researchers who he calls the “power theorists”. The ideas I’ve personally found most useful related to this line of thinking are </span><a href="https://www.journals.uchicago.edu/doi/abs/10.1086/226707"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">threshold models</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w28887/w28887.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">information</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=4779644"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cascades</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.amazon.com/Private-Truths-Public-Lies-Falsification/dp/0674707583"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">preference falsification</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/power-lies-trembling"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">coups as coordination games</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. My sense is that these provide good diagnoses of the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">problem</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of herd behavior (and that a better understanding of information cascades would have been very valuable for </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/s/NKTk9s4tZPiA4aySj/p/XrGkM3KFk7TTgRLNr"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">EA discussions of epistemic modesty</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) but don’t make much progress towards a theory of group rationality which would prevent this problem.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefc1bwfososwn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnc1bwfososwn">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The problem of group rationality is related to the problem of credit assignment in multi-agent reinforcement learning—a very difficult problem, since the effects of each agent’s behavior are mediated by other agents. My sense is that MARL researchers, like game theorists, don’t know how to describe the process of rationally choosing between possible equilibria, and instead focus on running agents and seeing what happens in practice. Conceptual clarity about multi-agent learning processes therefore seems rare—though two thinkers whose contributions I appreciate on these topics are </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1711.00363"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Andrew Critch</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wQKskToGofs4osdJ3/the-pando-problem-rethinking-ai-individuality"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jan</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/AvFAKAN4C4n6GTriR/the-artificial-self-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kulveit</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s worth noting that throughout this post, I’ve been particularly positive about the contributions of agent foundations researchers. This might just be an ingroup bias in my research taste. But my sense is that many agent foundations researchers are searching for a very deep and principled understanding of intelligence, to a much greater extent than researchers in other domains.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefivjbg29z46s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnivjbg29z46s">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I credit Yudkowsky for anchoring the field of agent foundations towards the pursuit of fundamental knowledge (e.g. </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2018/10/03/rocket-alignment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in this post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). A central example: agent foundations is much more rigorous about self-reference than other frameworks for describing intelligence (like ML theory, or active inference), as I explore in </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1nMufGPE2jIGKyZBCh67LGt8wPxvgJ1zCXWh9jLrX9jg/edit"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">my Understanding Intelligent Agency reading list</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn46jc3r0zq6c"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref46jc3r0zq6c">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I found </span><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20210121215131/https://theinexactsciences.github.io/Strategic%20Interactionism,%20pt%201.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this presentation by Suspended Reason</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on the history of sociology interesting, though I’m somewhat skeptical of the claims made in it.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnf7lcmbd4g7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreff7lcmbd4g7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In a different chapter Gintis discusses the “mixing problem”, an interesting idea I hadn’t encountered before. To give an example: the Nash equilibrium strategy in rock-paper-scissors is to play each with ⅓ probability. But given that your opponent is playing the equilibrium strategy, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">any</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> strategy gets equal payoff for you. So the equilibrium strategy sometimes isn’t more rational than any other strategy, even conditional on your opponent’s strategy.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fny665y8b63r9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefy665y8b63r9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zB4f7QqKhBHa5b37a/introduction-to-the-infra-bayesianism-sequence"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Infra-Bayesianism</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> claims to have made progress in this direction, though I haven’t yet figured out how to get any clear insight from it.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7wid7wypjso"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7wid7wypjso">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I particularly liked </span><a href="https://www.amazon.com/dp/0674135369"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fink’s Clinical Introduction to Lacanian Psychoanalysis</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which I found much more accessible than reading Lacan himself. I also appreciated </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/book-review-sadly-porn"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sadly, Porn</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.amazon.com/dp/1578636477"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Existential Kink</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which I think of as masculine- and feminine-flavored expositions of Lacanian ideas, respectively.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnc1bwfososwn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefc1bwfososwn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another idea which is related (but not directly linked to the power theorists, to my knowledge) is the concept of status. </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S1090513800000714"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Henrich and Gil-White</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> distinguish between two kinds of status: </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual_strategies_theory"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">prestige and dominance</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Meanwhile </span><a href="https://ageofem.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hanson and Simler</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> describe the ubiquity of status-seeking behavior in modern life, including self-deceptive status-seeking. I haven’t included these in the main text because neither of these don’t do much to define or ground the concept of status in a rational agent setting.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnivjbg29z46s"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefivjbg29z46s">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some central examples of work which engages with similar ideas, but doesn’t seem to be trying to build towards an elegant unified theory: </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.16984"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beyond Preferences in AI Alignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Zhi-Xuan et al., 2024), </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.19010"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A theory of appropriateness with applications to generative artificial intelligence</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Leibo et al., 2024), and </span><a href="https://www.full-stack-alignment.ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Full-stack alignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Edelman et al., 2025). As one illustration: Leibo et al. fill in the hard part of their theory by simply asking an LLM to categorize appropriateness, which </span><a href="https://x.com/richardmcngo/status/2107547999958384890?s=46"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">inspired me to post this meme</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DwHvtm8vLipAQPest/social-theory-and-agent-foundations#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Why you should run a rationality group at your university](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NAHnayEuQ2PdFRXHQ/why-you-should-run-a-rationality-group-at-your-university)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post was crossposted to the </span></i><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/WQsokJTWFep5coqim/why-you-should-run-a-rationality-group-at-your-university" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">EA Forum</span></i></a></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you enjoy LessWrong (and/or do EA/AI safety field building), you should consider starting a rationality reading group at your university. It’s fun, cheap, a good way to meet like-minded friends, and it can reach rationalist-ish people who’ve never encountered EA or AI safety.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Starting one can be as easy as grabbing a couple friends, picking a time, place, and reading, sending an email blast, and bringing food. The </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/fall-meetups-2026-call-for-meetup"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ACX Meetups Everywhere</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> call (</span><a href="https://docs.google.com/forms/d/e/1FAIpQLSfEGJI-UTmFHXk1mv_sK82X29CXnSrNf5xDb3Tr9YyL6ugA2g/viewform?usp=dialog"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">organizer form</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> due October 9th) can be a good way to find an initial group.</span></li></ul><hr /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Context: this post was loosely based on a memo originally written by Noah Birnbaum, and I (Carolanne Jiang) wrote most of the current version. “I” in this post refers to Carolanne, and “we” refers to Carolanne and Noah.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also, UChicago Rationality is still going! If you are a student at UChicago interested in joining, </span></i><a href="https://blueprint.uchicago.edu/organization/rationalitygroup"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">meetings this quarter</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are on Wednesdays from 6 :00 – 7:00 PM in the Reg. Noah and I are both no longer at UChicago–for more information, please reach out to Will Kyle at </span></i><a href="mailto:wkyle@uchicago.edu"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wkyle@uchicago.edu</span></a></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eco7BGbbrKkRi6mqH/new-uchicago-rationality-group"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">UChicago Rationality</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> was a huge success:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> it was very fun, a great place to make like-minded friends, and responsible for getting various people</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbc3an1j2et6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbc3an1j2et6">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> into AI safety and/or EA. If you're a frequent reader of LessWrong, an organizer for your university's EA or AI safety group, or are otherwise interested in rationality as a university student, it is possible that you'd have a great time running a rationality group at your school. This post is our strong encouragement for you to consider doing so, and guidance on how to do so effectively.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The backstory of UChicago Rationality</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A few years ago, </span><a href="https://noahbirnbaum.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Noah Birnbaum</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> was interested in rationality and field-building for EA and AI safety. He noticed that many rationality-curious people at UChicago had never really encountered EA or AI safety, so he started a reading group, partly to meet like-minded people and partly in the hope that the communities would start to overlap.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This took form as a weekly reading group of about 20 people, where we read </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/rationality" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the Sequences</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scott Alexander</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">'s blog posts, and other rationalist and adjacent writing. The ACX Meetups coordinator gave us funding to get sandwiches at a nearby restaurant, Scott kindly agreed to promote us on his blog, and the group grew so quickly that we sometimes had to cap attendance. For much of the club's history, the group hosted very high-quality conversations and brought together people who otherwise wouldn't have met, several of whom went on to get involved in EA and/or AI safety, including me (</span><a href="https://www.carolannejiang.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Carolanne</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More reasons on why you should consider running a rationality group</span></h1><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is fun (and low cost).</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7xe7z5e8f0u"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7xe7z5e8f0u">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> You get to read and talk about ideas you find interesting with friends and like-minded people, and you can get funding for e.g. food at club meetings (most clubs can get funding through their school, but there are </span><a href="https://www.rationalitymeetups.org/get-funding"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">other sources of funding </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to help make ambitious clubs possible).</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can build the community you wish existed! </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are often more rationalist-ish people around than you'd expect,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefre4tqmiwtub"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnre4tqmiwtub">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and a group gives them a way to find each other. At UChicago, the group surfaced many of these people, and I met lots of new friends there.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you're also interested in EA or AI safety community building</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (which Noah argues that </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/3aPCKsHdJqwKo2Dmt/why-you-should-become-a-university-group-organizer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">you should be</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), a rationality group is a great way to reach people who might get a lot out of EA or AI safety but haven't encountered them yet.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkho3g1b1q4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkho3g1b1q4">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Initial outreach in community building can be understood as sets of overlapping but distinct selection effects. Many forms of EA and AI safety outreach reaches people who are already somewhat interested, while a rationality group can attract people who share many of the same habits of mind but have never had a reason to look into EA or AI safety.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Come meet friends and talk about interesting ideas over food" is also a much lower barrier to entry (&amp; much lower commitment/investment) than an EA fellowship or AI safety event, so the invitation can go out to far more people.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you intend to start a group with the primary purpose of serving as a pipeline into EA or AI safety,</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefe5xq8c0e22"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fne5xq8c0e22">[5]</a></sup></span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> we strongly encourage you to make this intention clear to people</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and recognize in advance that (&amp; be okay with the fact that!) many folks will enjoy the rationality content but won’t be interested in EA/AIS.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7eksywzxkml"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7eksywzxkml">[6]</a></sup></span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Q: I am sold. How do I go about starting one?</span></h1><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is a call for ACX meetups everywhere organizers </span></b><a href="https://substack.com/@astralcodexten/p-215593613"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">right now</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If you’re interested in this round–you should </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/forms/d/e/1FAIpQLSfEGJI-UTmFHXk1mv_sK82X29CXnSrNf5xDb3Tr9YyL6ugA2g/viewform"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fill out the organizer form</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">October 9th</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This is a great opportunity to attract an initial group of people!</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3nlswsbfkhj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3nlswsbfkhj">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (The post includes also useful information about how to host your first meetup)</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You could also start by thinking of it as “a group of friends hanging out and reading interesting things together.”</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It doesn’t have to be some big, ambitious club: just grab a couple of friends, pick a time, a place, and a reading, send out an email blast, and bring food. That’s pretty much all there is to a minimal (and excellent!) rationality club.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxj4e63uzwyn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxj4e63uzwyn">[8]</a></sup></span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(If you want to be more ambitious, there are lots of general resources about starting university groups. </span><a href="https://opticforecasting.notion.site/Masterdoc-of-Forecasting-Club-Materials-08d69e3f7483436aadbfc84703ae274d"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is one I find particularly helpful and clear. It was originally written about starting forecasting groups, but lots of content here can be adapted to rationality groups, including </span><a href="https://opticforecasting.notion.site/Finding-co-organizers-19588c57094648b9834d53b7c34d9271"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">how to find co-organizers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://opticforecasting.notion.site/General-outreach-guide-d5d06c92f8864cff808c7ca91ef2508f"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">general outreach guide</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://opticforecasting.notion.site/General-tips-for-great-event-logistics-6b48c9d3f6cb4b51a10f64405a0e58d4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">general tips for great event logistics</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://opticforecasting.notion.site/How-to-do-email-effectively-826b9899ab6942eca2bd4ae84ccac982"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">how to send emails</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, etc.)</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you’re interested in starting a group and/or have more questions, please feel more than welcome to </span><a href="https://www.carolannejiang.com/contact.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reach out to me</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or </span><a href="http://rationalitymeetups.org"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Skyler</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> directly.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix </span></h1><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Counters arguments to why a rationality group might not contribute to field building for AI safety and/or EA</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the time of the UChicago Rationality group, LessWrong had somewhat less AI safety content compared to now, so perhaps most people interested in reading LW have already gone into AI safety.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Empirically, this does not seem to be the case; there seems to still be lots of talented rationalist-ish people not in AI safety. However, there may be reasons why they’re not in AI safety unrelated to the lack of opportunities, but I don’t think this should be a major concern. In addition, I believe this effect is likely to be minimal, as many college aged students have not fully decided what they want to do with their careers.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0sea7dk8e78"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0sea7dk8e78">[9]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The discussions were particularly good because there was good facilitation/talent scouting, which may not be easy to replicate.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We have some thoughts about how to make discussions go well, (e.g. the discussion should be very focused on specific points &amp; argumentation), and would be happy to write more about this if there is interest.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe UChicago has a disproportionately large number of people interested in rationalist-ish things</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This seems relatively unlikely. To the extent that the concentration of rationalist-ish people may vary across universities, we expect it to depend more on factors like school size, academic culture, and the strength of related communities, etc. than on anything unique to UChicago. In addition, even if interest is somewhat concentrated, a good group could work from a handful of engaged people.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to Skyler Crossman and Saul Munn for comments and suggestions, and Noah Birnbaum for the initial memo and feedback. Errors remain my own.</span></i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbc3an1j2et6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbc3an1j2et6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Over 2 years, we had ~20 people at most meetings (with frequent new faces), and ~150 on the mailing list. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By Noah’s rough estimate, the group was responsible for something like 10–30% of the people who got involved in UChicago EA, people who likely wouldn’t have found it otherwise, while taking only about 1.5–2 hours a week of the main organizer’s time.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More broadly, about 15% of</span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/s/5jbDGmiAMSWZ5k3mb/p/r8XoHhKKzmQgxm2Lf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> EA Survey 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> respondents first heard of EA via ACX or LessWrong, and about 20–25% cite each as important for getting involved (somewhat lower if you exclude respondents who found the survey through ACX). In</span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/rAqKSSXankvys2Fzu/the-case-for-ai-safety-capacity-building-work#:~:text=In%202023%2C%2060%25%20of%20respondents%20listed%20a%20capacity%2Dbuilding%20program%20or%20organization%20that%20our%20team%20was%20funding%20in%20their%20top%20four%20influences%2C%20with%20the%20most%20common%20being%20university%20groups%20(listed%20by%2025%25%20of%20respondents)"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Coefficient Giving’s 2023 survey</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of people working on (or likely to work on) AI safety and other catastrophic risks, university groups were the most common top-4 career influence (25%), and Eliezer Yudkowsky’s writing was listed by 11%.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7xe7z5e8f0u"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7xe7z5e8f0u">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">UChicago rationality took 1.5–2 hours a week of the main organizer’s time. This mostly looked like: picking a piece of writing, maybe re-reading it, booking a room, sending an email to the group, personally texting folks to join (which is very effective), and making a sandwich order. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Further more, if you have two concurrent reading groups that are 2h each and require 30m of preparation from you, that’s ~5h/week of total work (plus maybe another 30-60m of admin work, eg. paperwork with the university). </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnre4tqmiwtub"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefre4tqmiwtub">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Empirically, LessWrong, ACX, and related blogs have lots of readers, a non-trivial amount of whom are currently in university, and there aren’t that many universities out there. So, it is probably quite likely that there are a few at your school.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anecdotally, we just keep finding rationalist-ish people and Scott Alexander readers who didn’t know about any related groups at UChicago. They were mostly all excited to come to the rationality group.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkho3g1b1q4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkho3g1b1q4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Counter arguments: see appendix</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fne5xq8c0e22"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefe5xq8c0e22">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Noah started the group explicitly with the goal of getting people into EA while stating his intention as such.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7eksywzxkml"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7eksywzxkml">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If this is your goal, you may want to make sure that your successors are aligned to these goals. Alternatively, if you just want to enjoy running a reading group and don’t care about encouraging people to take action, that is fine as well. (and reading groups are great fun!)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3nlswsbfkhj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3nlswsbfkhj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We hosted 2 years of ACX meetups at UChicago, both of which were very well attended.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxj4e63uzwyn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxj4e63uzwyn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Less related to why you should start a rationality club in particular: consider gathering friends to read whatever you’ve been meaning to get around to, or whatever you enjoy and want to talk about with others! </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eg. Future of Humanity Institute reading group (cc </span><a href="https://lydia.ml/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lydia Nottingham</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> &amp; </span><a href="http://saulmunn.com"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Saul Munn</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), Joe Carlsmith reading group (cc </span><a href="http://saulmunn.com"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Saul Munn</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), Global Priorities Institute reading group, Forethought reading group, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/groups/LK6GNnKp8PDCkqcxx"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sequences reading group</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (cc Lighthaven), or </span><a href="https://partiful.com/e/kAmPqiZHGiPniYl9PiTK"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://partiful.com/e/z5RSdNdxgAKh7RwsONuz"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">book</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://jojiao.com/#:~:text=Recently,-My%20friend%20Keshav"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">club</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in general (cc </span><a href="https://jojiao.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jo Jiao</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), etc.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0sea7dk8e78"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0sea7dk8e78">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/screwtape"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Skyler</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> adds: many people find the rationalist community (or AI Safety or EA) in university, around 18~22. It's typically a time when one starts reading more broadly and bumping into more ideas and interest groups</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NAHnayEuQ2PdFRXHQ/why-you-should-run-a-rationality-group-at-your-university#comments">Discuss</a>

## Engineering

### [Fragments: October  4](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-04.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>In response to my <a href="https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html">last fragments</a> (probably the bit about us worrying if LLMs have consciousness when we when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience) <a href="https://x.com/metalanguage_os/status/2104934457333707058">“Metalanguage” replied</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>we shipped the id and forgot the superego. classic software lifecycle.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I don’t know what was on their mind, but their post immediately made me think of the classic 1956 movie Forbidden Planet. Plenty of sci-fi, and other literature, have explored humans creating technology with unintended behavior, going back at least to Mary Shelly. But that movie was particularly influential on sci-fi film-making and in the heart of its story is what happens when we nurture a thinking machine.</p>

<p>I use the term “nurture” here deliberately. We talk of building software, but building implies a degree of determinism. When we build a bridge, or a locomotive, we expect it to behave in a controlled and well-understood manner. That’s a difference in degree to how we cultivate plants in our garden, or nurture young children. One of the challenges of working with these systems is understanding what has changed in this shift from building a computational system to nurturing an inferential one, and how our processes need to change in response.</p>

<p>We get unintended behavior with deterministic building: some bridges have collapsed, and our  computational systems often have bugs. But one difference is that when we find a bug in a computational system we can usually fix it. Even if we can’t, we can usually disable a component so the bug won’t do further harm. With inferential LLMs however, there is no such simple fix or disablement, which may lead us to the fate of the Krell.</p>

<p>(If you haven’t seen Forbidden Planet, it’s well worth watching. Yes, it shows it was made in the 1950s - with special effects, music, acting, and attitudes of that decade. But the story is solid, and its key theme is very relevant to the future we build with generative AI. Just don’t read about it in advance, it’s better to be immersed in the story without spoilers - although my memory of that experience is understandably hazy.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Many people who follow me also know my friend Ola Bini, who was my colleague at Thoughtworks for many years, and was living in Ecuador working as an independent software security expert. Sadly his time in Ecuador was dogged by a <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2025/04/six-years-dangerous-misconceptions-targeting-ola-bini-and-digital-rights-ecuador">bogus prosecution</a> by the authorities there. But things seemed to have settled down, and although not allowed to leave Ecuador, Ola was able to get on with his life.</p>

<p>Sadly that’s no longer the case as he <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/ola-bini-ordered-leave-ecuador-under-obscure-accusations">was deported from Ecuador on Friday:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>According to information released by his lawyer, Bini was intercepted by a car with four people who identified themselves as immigration agents. He was then taken to an immigration office without further information or a formal order from a competent authority. There, officials told Bini that his visa had been revoked but didn’t show any supporting document.</p>

  <p>Bini’s defense filed a habeas corpus to safeguard his freedom and prevent his deportation. Yet, Ecuadorian authorities affirmed that the developer represents a threat or risk to public security and the state structure, and must leave the country. The ground for deportation is a secret report which allegedly asserts that Bini committed acts against the security of Ecuador. The defense could not access its contents.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I was really worried for a while, since it wasn’t clear where he was going to be deported to. But <a href="https://x.com/olabini/status/2106671967944511885">he tweeted from Sweden</a>, so I’m thankful for that. But this is only a partial relief. Ola has spent thirteen years in Ecuador and made it his home. To be thrown out of your home for scant reason is a heavy thing to bear, and the officials who did that have committed a serious offense.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>DDD Europe have released the video of <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fndU_B5rmPE">Gien Verschatse interviewing Eric Evans and myself</a> at the conference in June. We start by talking about how we bonded over conceptual modeling in the late 1990s. The conversation quickly moves to AI, we note that it’s impossible to predict how such a big change will work out. We do expect that it will cause us to think about our work in different ways, but the change may well be liberating, it’s reinvigorated Eric’s love of programming.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>people are probably going to feel very frustrated by [the new way of thinking about software]… but when you get through that, there is a kind of a wonderful feeling of my brain’s been loosened up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Our background in agile planning helps with the uncertainty, as we are used to taking small steps and being attentive to feedback. We mull on the interplay of writing and thinking, in terms of both prose and code, and how its very much an iterative process of exploration and refinement - the same is true when we chat with our LLMs.</p>

<p>And don’t miss Eric’s important final tip.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/paulg/status/2106018287117345070">Paul Graham:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>There were a lot of things that only worked because there’s a limit to the rate at which humans can operate. We’re about to find out what all of them are, as they break.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The speculation continues about whether or not reading code will play a part in a software developer’s future. <a href="https://ghuntley.com/readable/?ref=geoffrey-huntley-newsletter">Geoffrey Huntley says</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>People are still saying, very loudly, that code should be readable so that humans can understand it. I no longer think that’s the goal.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Interestingly his example has the LLM explain a haskell function definition… by translating it to Python. Which, to me, suggests there is a role for code - just that LLM need not store code in the same form that it presents it to a reader. This is essentially the same idea as <a href="https://www.martinfowler.com/bliki/ProjectionalEditing.html">projectional editing</a>, which posits that the editable representation of software need not be the same as its storage representation.</p>

<p>Sam Ruby touches on this as he <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/25/Pencils-Down-Notation-Up.html">muses on a Rails World keynote</a>. He quotes DHH saying:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Rust is a good prompt compilation target for the moment, but so is C++. And soon assembler. Then microcode. Myopic to think we’re going to stop the agentic drill bit until it reaches computing bedrock.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He responds with:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The post leaves one question unasked, though: what sits at the top of the drill? What do we keep, edit and trust as the source of truth?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He carries out exercise of looking at some Rails software. Represented in Ruby/Rails and its about 60,000 tokens. Compiling it into C it turns into 4,000,000 tokens. That increase in token size will hamper the LLM, that still has to fit it into its context window, and even if it were to fit, figure out where to focus its attention. Sam points out reasons why, even absent a human reading it, it makes sense to represent the program in a higher-level language.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>what Rails becomes when agents write the code: the most compact, precise and conventional specification of a web application, whatever it ends up compiled to.</p>

  <p>Let the drill go as deep as it can. Just keep the notation at the top.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This all reminds me of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/what-is-code.html#CognitiveDebt">what Unmesh Joshi argued</a>: that code serves “two distinct
but intertwined purposes”: instructions to a machine, and a conceptual model of
the problem domain. After exploring how those change with LLMs he concludes:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The role of coding is not disappearing. But it is changing.</p>

  <p>As LLMs make code generation cheaper, the mechanical act of writing instructions becomes less central. What becomes more important is making the conceptual model explicit, discovering the right vocabulary, and refining that vocabulary through iteration, domain expertise, and feedback. This is also why programming languages continue to matter deeply. We are not meant to be passive reviewers of generated code. The act of writing code is itself part of our thinking.</p>

  <p>Code is still instructions for a machine. But it is also a model of understanding. In the LLM era, that second role becomes even more important. The future of coding is not just writing more code faster. It is building better conceptual models, better vocabularies, and better foundations on top of which both humans and LLMs can work.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In a later post, Sam <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/27/Partly-in-the-Right.html">pondered on how people are talking about the capabilities of agents</a> in a way that resembles the parable of the blind men and the elephant. We all only have only a partial view of this object and where it’s going. A theme for all of us:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The practical question isn’t whether agents are good. It’s this: for the task in front of you this week, where will the information come from, and what will check the result?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The Economist’s pithy summation of <a href="https://www.economist.com/finance-and-economics/2026/09/22/you-cant-trade-your-way-through-the-ai-apocalypse">investors concerns about the dangers of AI companies’ products</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It’s hard to celebrate an initial public offering that leads to a terminal public offing.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The news about the latest model from Google <a href="https://x.com/aipulseda1ly/status/2105394296815779861">is interesting</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Gemini 4 Argon has an insanely low hallucination rate on Artificial Analysis. 15%.</p>

  <p>Grok 4.7 is at 29%. GPT-6 Astra 45%. Opus 5.5 59%. Fable 5.1 69%.</p>

  <p>The only models below it barely answer anything. None of them get more than 15% right.</p>

  <p>It gets fewer answers right than Opus 5.5 on max, 50% against 66%. But when it doesnt know, it says so instead of making something up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Being clearer about what it doesn’t know, at a cost of getting less answers right, is definitely a trade-off I prefer.</p>

---

### [Principles for effective slides](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Not every presentation needs slides, but when they earn their place,
      they work as a visual channel that complements the speaker rather than
      replacing them. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> sets out the
      principles that follow from that idea &#x2014; control over the rate of knowledge
      exposition, tight coupling between slides and speaker, minimalism by
      default, visuals that say more than words can, and never sending your
      slides out in advance.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html">more…</a></p>
